NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 4 
Welcome
The National Insurance Commission (NIC) is pleased to 
present the 2024 Annual Report of the Insurance Industry 
in Ghana.
This report offers a comprehensive overview of the 
Ghanaian insurance sector, highlighting the performance 
of regulated entities through key performance indicators. 
The data and information presented herein are based 
on audited figures for 2024, with the exception of those 
entities that failed to submit their audited financial 
statements and Supervision Department Returns (SDRs) 
by the designated cut-off date.
Consequently, the figures for the following Life, Non-Life, 
Reinsurance, and Intermediary companies are not based 
on audited financial statements, as they failed to meet the 
submission deadline for this report:
Table 1: List of firms with data based on unaudited financial statements 
Life Insurers Non-Life Insurers Reinsurers Intermediaries
Pinnacle Insurance Company 
Limited (Donewell Life) Hollard Insurance Ghana Re Baobab Insurance Brokers Limited
GLICO Life Imperial General Assurance Worldwide Insurance Brokers Limited

 5 ANNUAL REPORT
on the Insurance Industry
Foreword
The insurance landscape in Ghana has consistently 
witnessed positive nominal growth for over a decade. 
However, despite this impressive trend, insurance 
penetration continues to hover under 2%, raising 
fundamental questions about the true impact and 
trajectory of this growth. This annual report, therefore, 
serves as a crucial benchmark, reflecting the 2024 annual 
performance for the industry, while setting the stage for a 
transformative future. 
In 2024, the insurance sector demonstrated impressive 
resilience amidst economic headwinds, showing a positive 
trend in both insurance revenue and total asset values in 
nominal terms. However, a deeper analysis reveals the 
pervasive impact of inflation. While the Life insurance 
sector saw a substantial nominal asset growth of 22%, 
real growth contracted to 1%, indicating that inflationary 
pressures largely eroded nominal gains. Similarly, Non-Life 
assets grew by 13% nominally but contracted to -9% in real 
terms, signifying a decrease in the real purchasing power 
of their assets. The Reinsurance sector, benefiting from its 
international business exposure, stood out with a robust 
44% nominal growth and an impressive 16% real growth, 
substantially outpacing inflation and increasing the real 
purchasing power of its assets. 
The implementation of International Financial Reporting 
Standards (IFRS 17) in 2024 brought significant changes 
to reporting standards, necessitating revisions across the 
industry and leading to some delays in submissions. 
Furthermore, the NIC made significant strides in 
strengthening the regulatory framework by issuing a 
comprehensive ESG guidelines, intensifying Risk-Based 
Supervision (RBS) with a focus on systemic risks, and 
developing a draft Risk-Based Capital (RBC) framework, 
aligned with global best practices, to enhance market 
resilience. Innovation remained a key focus, with six entities 
admitted into our regulatory sandbox, and the second 
annual Tech Forum successfully convened to explore 
technology as an enabler for market transformation, 
complemented by the development of a cybersecurity 
directive. 
The NIC also continued our Public Perception and 
Awareness and Confidence (PPAC) surveys, generating 
valuable insights to inform future regulatory policy.
Our Next Strategic Direction from 2025 
Recognizing the persistent challenge of low insurance 
penetration, the National Insurance Commission is 
embarking on a bold new strategic direction, encapsulated 
in its upcoming strategic plan. This plan is designed to 
not only consolidate past gains but also to significantly 
expand and sanitize the Ghanaian insurance market, while 
ensuring there is rapid growth and positioning of the NIC 
as a regulator of choice. 
Sanitizing the Insurance Market
A core pillar of the new strategic direction is the sanitization 
of the insurance market. We will intensify our focus on 
ensuring fair and adequate pricing for policies underwritten, 
and fair market conduct by working closely with all 
stakeholders. The NIC will be proactive and decisive in 
addressing practices that undermine market integrity, 
by always prioritizing the interests of policyholders and 
fostering a transparent and trustworthy environment. 
This includes rigorous enforcement of regulatory laws and 
requirements and taking appropriate measures against 
entities that fail to meet prudential or market standards. 
Growing the Insurance Market Using Non-Traditional 
Methods
To address the persistent challenge of low penetration, 
we are committed to growing the insurance market using 
non-traditional methods. This entails creating enabling 
environments that support innovative approaches and 
the utilization of technology. Inclusive insurance market 
development, leveraging InsurTechs, and promoting 
the adoption of digital solutions will be paramount. Our 
regulatory sandbox will continue to be a vital tool for testing 
and scaling new products, services, and business models 
that cater to the unserved and underserved populations, 
ensuring financial inclusion and market expansion beyond 
conventional channels. 
International Positioning
Finally, the NIC is poised to elevate Ghana’s standing 
on the global insurance stage through our international 
positioning strategy. We aim to establish Ghana as a 
premier destination for insurance business expansion 
across Africa. This involves aligning our regulatory 
framework with international best practices, fostering 
a competitive and attractive market for international 
investors, and showcasing Ghana’s potential as a hub for 
innovation and growth in the African insurance landscape. 
Our engagement with international bodies and adherence 
to global standards, such as the IAIS’s Insurance Capital 
Standards, will underscore this commitment. 
In Conclusion
2025 will see the beginning of the new strategic plan for 
the NIC. A number of initiatives will be launched to set 
the tone for the transformation of the insurance market in 
Ghana 

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 6 
Chairperson’s Remarks
Introduction
I am pleased to present the 2024 annual report of the 
National Insurance Commission (NIC) Ghana. This report 
details our internal activities and market performance 
throughout 2024, all in pursuit of the NIC’s objective of 
fostering a fair, safe, efficient, and stable insurance market 
in Ghana.
To achieve this overarching objective, the NIC is focused 
on supporting sustainable market development, ensuring 
customer protection, encouraging financial inclusion, and 
promoting market growth to boost insurance penetration 
across the nation. 
Upholding Market Stability and Consumer Confidence
Protecting insurance customers and consumers is 
paramount. We are resolute in our commitment to 
ensuring the sector remains stable, secure, and healthy 
through varying economic conditions. This builds public 
confidence, demonstrating that insurance truly serves as a 
safeguard, offering protection and cushion during adversity 
and misfortune.
The NIC will continue to be proactive and focused, 
responding swiftly to systemic risks that could disrupt the 
insurance market. Our core role is to ensure all regulated 
entities are financially sound, healthy, and consistently 
operate in the best interests of policyholders. We continually 
enforce adherence to regulatory laws and requirements. 
Should a regulated entity fail to meet prudential or market 
requirements, the NIC will act decisively with appropriate 
measures, always prioritizing the interests of policyholders 
and considering the entity’s nature, size, and complexity.
2024 Financial Sector Overview
The financial sector in 2024 faced notable challenges. The 
Ghana Cedi experienced a year-end depreciation of 19% 
against the US Dollar, trading at GHS14.89.
The NIC actively participated in the Financial Stability 
Council’s (FSC) activities, collaborating closely with other 
financial sector regulators. This engagement aims to 
promote overall financial system stability and collectively 
address shared system-wide risks. Specific areas of 
concern and focus for the FSC included:
1. Assessing the financial sector’s preparedness for disruptions.
2. Conglomerate regulation.
3. Macroprudential policy settings.
4. The broader economic environment and other relevant factors.
Market Performance: Assets Growth and Insurance Revenue Analysis
Despite economic headwinds, the insurance sector 
demonstrated impressive performance in 2024, showing a 
positive trend in insurance revenue and total asset values. 
The table below highlights both nominal and real growth 
in total assets:

To provide context for these figures, Ghana experienced 
a high-inflation environment between 2023 and 2024. 
The Consumer Price Index (CPI) increased from 200.5 to 
248.3, indicating an approximate inflation rate of 23%. For 
this analysis, the 2022 CPI of 162.8 was used as the base 
CPI. This high inflation rate is critical for understanding the 
significant difference between nominal and real growth.

Sector-Specific Asset Performance
Life Insurance Sector
While the Life insurance sector recorded a substantial 
nominal growth of 22% in total assets from 2023 to 
2024 (measured in current Ghana Cedis), the real growth 
contracted to 1%. This indicates that nearly all nominal 
gains were eroded by inflation, meaning the sector barely 
increased the actual purchasing power of its assets. Growth 
in nominal terms was largely nullified by rising prices, with 
very little real expansion in the asset base of life insurers.
Non-Life Insurers
Non-life assets grew by 13% in nominal terms, a slower rate 
than the Life sector. However, the concerning real growth 
of -9% indicates that the nominal increase was insufficient 
to offset high inflation. In real terms, the purchasing 
power of assets held by non-life insurance companies 
significantly decreased between 2023 and 2024, signifying 
a contraction in its real asset base.
Reinsurers
The Reinsurance sector demonstrated the strongest 
performance, showing a robust 44% nominal growth. 
More impressively, its 16% real growth indicates that the 
sector not only expanded significantly in nominal terms 
but also substantially outpaced inflation. This led to a 
considerable increase in the real purchasing power of its 
assets, suggesting the sector is thriving even in a highinflation environment. This strong performance is largely 
attributable to the international nature of its business, with 
substantial foreign assets on reinsurers’ balance sheets.
Insurance Revenue
For Insurance Revenue, we saw a departure in the financial 
statements of gross premiums to insurance revenue in 
line with the International Financial Reporting Standards 
(IFRS 17). Non-Life Insurance Revenue increased from 
GHS 3.9 billion to GHS 5.0 billion, representing 28% while 
Life Insurance Revenue increased to GHS 2.33 billion from 
GHS 1.71 billion, representing a nominal growth of 36%. 
Reinsurers experienced a surge in Insurance Revenue 
to GHS 897 million from GHS 655 million, representing 
37% growth. While these appear significant in nominal 
terms, the real growth using CPI to deflate the nominal 
growth resulted in 3.32%, 10.43% and 10.56% growth for 
the Non-Life, Life and Reinsurers segments of the market 
respectively. This means that there is a need to shift 
paradigm by working to expand the insurance market.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 8 
Chairperson’s Remarks
Key Activities 2024
In 2024, the NIC undertook a series of key activities in pursuit of its supervisory oversight, strategic plan, and priorities. 
Highlights of these activities are outlined below:
International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS 17) Implementation
The year 2024 marked the implementation of IFRS 
17, bringing a complete change in the measurement, 
presentation, and disclosure requirements for regulated 
insurance and reinsurance companies. This necessitated 
significant revisions in regulatory reporting formats and 
industry systems to align with the new data requirements, 
which resulted in some general delays in the submission 
of 2024 reports.
Environmental, Social, and Governance (ESG) Guidelines
Environmental, Social, and Governance (ESG) issues have 
become a significant concern for financial sector regulators 
globally. The NIC conducted capacity-building training 
sessions and held industry stakeholder discussions on the 
critical need to embed ESG principles across the insurance 
value chain of regulated entities. In 2024, the NIC issued 
ESG directives that provide key guidance for integrating 
ESG requirements within the insurance industry. This 
initiative is expected to support the market in establishing 
robust ESG practices across their operations.
Risk-Based Supervision (RBS)
Over the past few years, the NIC has intensified its 
supervisory focus on systemic and large risks that could 
destabilize the insurance industry. The current supervisory 
model is a Risk-Based Approach. A key element of RBS 
is assessing the inherent risks associated with insurance 
companies’ significant activities and evaluating the 
existence and effectiveness of control functions in mitigating 
these risks. In 2024, the NIC focused its on-site inspections 
on the RBS approach, which will lead to the completion 
of each insurer’s risk rating. A risk-based approach allows 
the NIC to focus scarce regulatory resources on risks that 
are systemic and have a larger impact on the insurance 
industry. The NIC anticipates completing a full cycle of the 
RBS approach with each regulated entity by the close of 
2026, ensuring that subsequent risk assessments are fully 
based on this approach.
Risk-Based Capital (RBC) Framework
In line with the risk-based approach, and with the support 
of the World Bank, the NIC developed and released a draft 
Risk-Based Capital (RBC) framework for the insurance 
industry. This framework aims to enhance the financial 
health and resilience of the insurance market. The draft 
RBC framework, expected to be issued in 2026, aligns 
with global best practices, including the Insurance Capital 
Standards (ICS) of the IAIS, the Canadian model, and 
some African RBC models. The NIC conducted several 
industry engagements to gather feedback on the draft 
framework, ensuring it is homegrown and tailored to our 
local context. This framework is expected to strengthen 
industry’s resilience and its capacity to withstand adverse 
shocks.
Sandbox, Tech Forum, and Cybersecurity
In line with the NIC’s mandate to leverage innovation and 
InsurTechs to support market development, six (6) entities 
were admitted into the regulatory sandbox environment. 
The sandbox provides the NIC with an opportunity 
to test the impact of such innovations in a controlled 
environment. The NIC also successfully held the second 
annual Tech Forum during this period. The forum set 
the agenda for market participants to discuss industry 
challenges and explore how collectively; the industry can 
leverage technology and innovation as enablers for market 
transformation.
To ensure safeguards around the adoption of technology 
and innovation, the NIC issued a cyber security directive 
for the industry to provide sufficient safeguards against 
risks from cyber related exposures.
Surveys on market 
Public Perception and Awareness and Confidence (PPAC) 
was also conducted in the year as the fifth of the series of 
surveys. The aim is to generate ideas to support regulatory 
policy by understanding public perceptions, their level 
of awareness of the insurance industry, and its products 
and services. The feedback from the survey, which mainly 
showed improvements, will be used to support policy.

 9 ANNUAL REPORT
on the Insurance Industry
Outlook for the Sector
The insurance market requires sanitization and expansion 
to consolidate the gains that have so far been made. This 
requires leadership and direction, which the NIC shall 
endeavor to push to develop the market. Primarily, the 
NIC would pay attention to insurance pricing to ensure 
that adequate premiums are charged for policies that 
are underwritten. The NIC will engage its stakeholders 
to sanitize the market. Additionally, the market requires 
expansion to cover the unserved and underserved 
populations. The NIC intends to create enablers that will 
support the use of non-traditional channels and models to 
support the expansion of the insurance market. Inclusive 
insurance market development and the use of tech and 
innovation will be priority for the NIC. Finally, the NIC 
will work towards creating an international reputation for 
insurance business in Ghana by positioning the market as 
the destination for African insurance market expansion.
Conclusion
I would like to extend my heartfelt appreciation to the 
newly constituted board, of which I serve as chairperson, 
and to the new executive team for their dedication and 
commitment to change the narrative. Ours, is to commit 
with the mandate of His Excellency the President of Ghana, 
John Dramani Mahama to reset Ghana. Together, we will 
continue to uphold the highest standards of governance, 
create the enablers for growth, leverage technology and 
innovation, and enhance stakeholder engagement to 
safeguard the integrity and stability of the insurance sector 
in Ghana.
Chairperson’s Remarks

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 12 
BOARD OF DIRECTORS
MS. ABENAA KESSEWAA BROWNis a professional with over 35 years of combined local and international 
experience in Accounting, Banking, Finance, Governance, Learning, People, Technology, and 
Transformation strategies. She is a Certified Public Accountant and member of ICAG. As an advanced 
Facilitator, her focus is on design and delivery of Leadership, Management, Talent, Diversity, 
Performance and Change programs. She is the past Principal of the National Banking College, 
Ghana and is currently the Executive Director for AYA institute of finance and management. 
She is a mother of two children. 
MR. MICHAEL KOFI ANDOH is a seasoned insurance regulator with over 24 years of experience in the 
Ghanaian insurance industry. He was appointed the Ag. Commissioner of Insurance following the 
retirement of Prof. Dr. Justice Ofori. 
He rose through the ranks from an Inspections Manager from June 2000 to January 2003 when he was 
promoted to become the Financial Controller of the Commission. He became the Head of Supervision from 
January 2006 to September 2017 when he was appointed the Deputy Commissioner of Insurance. Prior to 
joining the NIC, he worked for three years with KPMG. 
Mr. Michael Andoh has been involved in a number of projects to develop the insurance market 
and improve access to insurance in Ghana. He has been actively involved in the design 
and implementation of a Risk Based Supervisory framework for the Ghanaian insurance 
industry. He has also played a leading role in the design and implementation of Ghana’s 
microinsurance regime. 
Mr. Michael Kofi Andoh holds a BSc in Business Administration from the University of Ghana 
Business School, and an MBA from the University of Leicester in the United Kingdom. He 
is also a Fellow of the Certified Chartered Accountants, (FCCA) and an Associate of the 
Chartered Insurance Institute (ACII) in the United Kingdom.
MR. SAMPSON AKLIGOH is an Economist with over 15 years of experience in Policy and Strategy, Investment 
Banking, Asset Management, Economic & Financial modeling, Economic theory, and practice. He is 
currently the Director of the Financial Sector Division of the Ministry of Finance.
Prior to this role, he worked in an advisory and asset management firm in the financial services industry as an 
economist. He was the Managing Director of InvestCorp, a financial service firm in Accra.
In addition, he served as a Vice President at Databank where he played leading roles as 
an Economist, Head of Research and as a Fixed Income Strategist. He also worked at SIC 
Financial Services Limited and with ADC African Development Corporation AG in Frankfurt, 
which was acquired by Atlas Mara in 2014. Mr. Akligoh previously served as an adjunct lecturer 
in International Economics at Ashesi University College in 2014.
He holds a BA in Economics and Law (First Class Honours) from the Kwame Nkrumah 
University of Science and Technology in Ghana, and a Master’s degree in Economic Policy 
and Corporate Strategy from the Maastricht School of Management in the Netherlands.

BOARD OF DIRECTORS
MR. EMMANUEL AMOFA is a Lawyer by profession and a member of the Ghana Bar Association (GBA) 
with 25 years’ legal experience. His areas of expertise and interest are Corporate Law, Investment Law, 
Commercial Law, Civil Litigation, Land Law, International Business Transactions, Negotiations, Domestic 
and International Commercial Arbitration, and Alternate Dispute Resolution (ADR). He is the founder and Managing 
Partner of Amofa & Partners and the Administrator of the Ghana Arbitration Centre since its incorporation. He was 
awarded a certificate in International Commercial Arbitration by the International Law Institute, Washington DC 
and Georgetown University, Washington DC.
He is a panel member of Arbitrators on International Centre for Settlement of Investment 
Disputes, Washington, DC. He is a lecturer in Alternative Dispute Resolution at the Ghana 
School of Law and an adjunct lecturer at the University of Ghana Faculty of Law, Legon.
He is the country contributor for Ghana on Contemporary Law on Arbitration in Ghana 
published in Arbitration in Africa: A Practioner’s Guide, Kluwer Law International, 2013.
Authored the Chapter on Ghana Arbitration Centre in Onyema Emilia (gen ed.). The 
transformation of Arbitration in Africa: The Role of Arbitral Institutions.
MR. FRANKLIN OWUSU ASAFO ADJEI is a Chartered Accountant and a Chartered Insurer. He has over 
30 years of experience in Accountancy, Finance, Insurance, and Management, with good knowledge and 
insight into the workings and dynamics of the Stock Exchange, Fund Management, and Banking. 
He was an Alumnus of the University of Ghana Business School and Emile Woolf College, 
London. He had previously been a Full-time Lecturer at the Ghana Institute of Management 
and Public Administration (GIMPA) Business School. He had previously lectured at Central 
University, Methodist University College, and IPS (now UPSA) as a Part-time Lecturer. He was 
once an Examiner for the Institute of Chartered Accountants, Ghana, in Auditing, Financial 
Accounting, and Financial Management at Stage 2, Stage 3 and Stage 4, respectively, at 
different times. 
He retired as the Chief Executive Officer (CEO) of Best Assurance Company Limited in May 
2020. He is currently a Part-time Lecturer, at the University of Ghana Business School.
PROF. BILL BUENAR PUPLAMPU is an academic and a practicing Chartered Psychologist with specialization 
in Occupational Psychology and Organizational Behaviour. He has over 25 years of consulting and practice 
experience in the application of Organizational Psychology to Corporate Governance, Human Capital 
Development, Human Resource Management, and Organisational Behaviour.
He holds a Ph.D. in Organizational Behavior and an MSc. in Occupational Psychology both from the University of 
Ghana.
He has served on various Boards including Ghana Psychology Council, Merchant Bank, and 
Databank Educational Investment Fund. He is a member of the National Development 
Planning Commission and a Fellow of the Ghana Academy of Arts and Sciences. 
He has taught at the University of East London and the University of Westminster (both in the 
UK), and the University of Ghana where he was the Head of the Department of Organisation 
and HRM at the Business School. He served as Dean of the Central Business School (Central 
University) and was later appointed as the Vice-Chancellor for Academic Affairs.
He is currently the Vice-Chancellor of Central University College.

She has held senior management level positions in the areas of business administration, public policy, 
investment banking and non-bank financial services, regulatory compliance in the securities industry and now 
works in the telecommunications industry.
Mrs. Striggner-Quartey’s strong professional and communication skills have been successfully 
harnessed to build productive relationships and further both organizational and personal 
goals.
She is currently the Director of Legal and External Affairs of Vodafone Ghana.
MRS. NAA SHORMEH GYANG (SECRETARY) is a Barrister at Law and a Solicitor of the Supreme Court 
of Ghana with over 25 years of Corporate Law practice. She is a member of the Ghana Bar Association 
(GBA). Mrs. Gyang started her legal career in 1990 with Legal Aid Ghana, as a Legal Aid Officer. 
Prior to joining the NIC in 2018, she worked with Merchant Bank Ghana Limited (now Universal Merchant Bank) 
in key management positions after having risen through the ranks when she joined the Bank 
in 1991 as a Legal Officer. 
Mrs. Gyang provides support for the Legal, Regulatory and Governance Functions of the 
Commission.
She is currently the Head of the Legal, Human Resource and Administration Department of 
the NIC.

products, marking an increase compared to the thirty-one 
(31) products approved in 2023. Out of these, twentyseven (27) of the products approved were Life products, 
whilst nine (9) were Non-Life products. The table below 
summarizes the products approved in 2024:

The rate of resolution of complaints by the NIC has

NIC is relentless in its efforts to ensure that the protection

Regular meetings, facilitated by the NIC, have been and

approach not only ensures speedy resolution but also 
enhances the overall customer experience. 
Also, in line with its commitment to improve customer 
service and ensure fair treatment of policyholders, 
the NIC launched its online Complaints Management 
System (CMS) on February 27, 2024. The CMS enables 
policyholders to lodge complaints conveniently via mobile 
devices without visiting NIC offices. The platform provides 
real-time, automated, and user-friendly access to the NIC 
and regulated entities.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 20 
In 2024, the NIC, in its commitment to streamline the 
regulatory framework of the Ghanaian insurance market, 
issued several pivotal regulations and directives. These are 
direct exercise of the NIC’s mandate and are designed to 
give full impetus to the Insurance Act, 2021 (ACT 1061),
ensuring a robust, stable, and consumer-centric insurance 
sector.
The key regulations and directives issued during the year include:
Insurance Regulations, 2024 (L.I. 2488): This foundational 
Legislative Instrument provides comprehensive legal 
backing and detailed operational guidelines for various 
aspects of the insurance business in Ghana, further 
elaborating on the provisions of the Insurance Act, 2021. 
It aims to enhance regulatory oversight, promote market 
discipline, and ensure adherence to best practices across 
the industry.
Directives
1. Insurers Directive (Governance and Risk 
Management, Reinsurance, and Conduct of 
Business): The Directive outlines technical 
instructions for insurers and reinsurers pertaining 
to their governance and risk management, 
conduct of business, and reinsurance 
arrangements. An accompanying set of 
guidance notes is included in the Directives to 
facilitate its understanding.
2. Directive on the Underwriting of Bond Business 
in Ghana: This specialized directive provides 
specific guidelines and requirements for insurers 
engaged in bond underwriting. Its purpose is 
to ensure sound practices, appropriate risk 
assessment, and adequate capital backing for 
this particular class of business, and a general 
guidance on bonds that are proscribed for the 
insurance industry thereby safeguarding the 
integrity of bond class of business.
3. Sandbox Directive: Reflecting the NIC’s 
commitment to fostering innovation within a 
controlled environment, the Sandbox Directive 
establishes a regulatory sandbox framework. 
This allows for the testing of new and innovative 
insurance products, services, and business 
models under relaxed regulatory requirements, 
facilitating technological advancement while 
mitigating potential risks in line with the 
requirements on innovative insurance under the 
Insurance Act.
4. Cyber Security Directive: In recognition of the 
increasing digital risks facing the financial sector, 
this directive mandates robust cybersecurity 
measures for all regulated insurance entities. It 
aims to protect sensitive customer data, ensure 
the integrity of digital operations, and enhance 
the industry’s resilience against cyber threats.
Directives/Guidelines

STATUTORY FUNDS
Motor Compensation Fund
The Motor Compensation Fund operates under Section 
228 of the Insurance Act, 2024 (Act 1061). The objective of 
the Fund is to compensate persons who suffer injury or the 
dependents of persons who die through a motor accident 
and who are unable to obtain compensation from any 
insurance company. 
The Award Sub-Committee of the Motor Compensation 
Fund (MCF) met on 18 occasions, during which 133 
applicants were awarded a total of GH¢ 2,345,046. 
The average claim per award demonstrated an upward 
trend in 2024. Despite a decline in the number of awards, 
the increased average indicates a movement towards fewer 
but more substantial claims. The table below provides a 
summary of the awards granted under the MCF.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 22 
Insurance Education Fund
The Insurance Act, 2021 (Act 1061) established the Insurance Education Fund with the general aim of providing training 
and education on insurance to the insurance industry and the public.
The Insurance Education Fund is managed by the Insurance Education Fund Committee made up of representatives of 
the NIC and regulated insurance entities that make contribution to the Fund. The role of the Committee is to advise the 
NIC on the management of the Fund including disbursements.
Objectives of the Insurance Education Fund
The key objectives of the Insurance Education Fund 
are to provide financial support to:
• The Ghana Insurance College for training 
persons working in the insurance industry 
with particular attention in the areas of 
competence and ethics.
• Educate Ghanaians on insurance matters 
through the NIC, the Ghana Insurance 
College and any other relevant institution. 
The sources of money to the Insurance Education 
Fund include contributions from licensed insurers 
and reinsurers and funds from funding agencies and 
other sources approved by the Commission.
In the year 2024, the Insurance Education Fund 
carried out several training and public education 
activities. These included training of the insurers, 
reinsurers and insurance intermediaries on IFRS17, 
shareholders, board members and chief executive 
officers of insurance companies on ESG. Training 
was also carried out for journalists across the country 
on insurance matters and reports. In the year 2024, 
the Insurance Education disbursed a total amount 
of GHS4,219,979.00 on industry training and public 
education. 
Agriculture Insurance Fund
The NIC under the Insurance Act 2021, (Act 1061) established the Agricultural Insurance Fund (AIF) under section 245. 
The establishment of the Fund underscores the importance of agriculture to the development of the Ghanaian economy. 
Providing insurance coverage for climate adaptation risks faced by farmers is essential in building an inclusive insurance 
market. Such insurances will impact positively on agricultural productivity, security for farmers, stable income for farmers 
and alleviate poverty among farmers in Ghana. 
The objectives of the Agricultural Insurance Fund are to provide financial resources:
• To subsidise agricultural insurance 
premiums. 
• To train agricultural extension officers 
and other persons who are required to 
disseminate agricultural insurance to 
farmers. 
• For the acquisition of equipment for 
relevant agencies that may generate 
data in the development of agricultural 
insurance products. 
• To build the capacity of state institutions to 
provide consistent and reliable data for the 
development of insurance products. 
• To invest in technology to disseminate 
agricultural insurance to farmers. 
• For any other activity related to the 
development of agricultural insurance. 
The AIF seeks to source funds to provide subsidies for 
insurers that provide Agriculture insurance to farmers and 
persons across the agricultural value chain. There has 
been no major contribution to the Agricultural Insurance 
Fund to enable the fund to carry out its mandate. 
In its effort to provide funding for the implementation 
of the Agricultural Insurance Fund, the NIC collaborated 
with Policylink Ghana and other development partners 
including Global Shield Solution Platform (GSSP), 
International Labour Organization (ILO), United Nations 
Development Programme (UNDP) and United States 
Agency for International Development (USAID) to assist 
in securing funds to the Agricultural Insurance Fund. 
By operationalizing the Agricultural Insurance Fund, farmers 
in Ghana stands to benefit immensely since they will now 
have cover over the risks that they face in their operations.

on the Insurance Industry
Client Rescue Fund
The Client Rescue Fund as established under section 235 of the Insurance Act 1061 is to provide compensation to 
policyholders of insurers and reinsurers that are wound up as insolvent. The Client Rescue Fund is administered by the 
Client Rescue Fund Committee with representatives from the NIC and the Insurance Companies that make contributions 
to the fund.
Over the years, the Client Rescue Fund has compensated policyholders of some insolvent insurance companies. The 
Fund has also supported the statutory management process of companies that are under enforcement.
1. The Client Rescue Fund in the year 2024 paid 
GHS2,000,000.00 as compensation to the 
policyholders of companies under enforcement 
process.
2. In addition, the Fund provided support to 
the statutory management process of the 
companies under enforcement by paying the 
statutory management and legal fees to a tune 
of GHS1,021,129.00 in the year 2024.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 24 
Economic 
Review Report 
for 2024

 25 ANNUAL REPORT
on the Insurance Industry
Global economy grew at 3.3 percent in 2024 compared to 
3.0 percent in 2023, even though a few countries, especially 
low-income, have seen sizeable downside growth 
revisions, often because of increased conflicts. However, 
notable revisions have taken place beneath the surface, 
with upgrades for the United States offsetting downgrades 
to those for advanced economies, particularly the largest 
European countries. Similarly, disruptions to production 
and shipping of commodities, especially oil, conflicts, civil 
unrest and extreme weather events have led to downward 
revisions to the outlook of some emerging markets (Middle 
East and Central Asia and that for the Sub-Saharan 
Africa). These have been compensated for by upgrades 
to the forecast for emerging Asia, where surging demand 
for semiconductors and electronics, driven by significant 
investments in artificial intelligence, has bolstered growth. 
Following a reopening rebound in 2022, growth in 
advanced economies remarkedly slowed in 2023 and is 
projected to remain steady, oscillating between 1.7 percent 
and 1.8 percent until 2029. Advanced countries grew at 
1.8 percent in 2024 as against a growth of 1.7 percent in 
2023. The United States of America’s economy grew at 2.8 
percent in 2024 compared to 2023 growth of 2.9 percent. 
The United Kingdom’s economy saw an elevation in its 
growth to 1.1 percent in 2024 compared to 0.3 percent 
growth in 2023. Germany’s economy improved from a 
negative 0.3 percent in 2023 to negative 0.2 percent in 
2024. The Emerging Markets and Developing Economies 
experienced a marginal drop in growth of 4.3 percent 
in 2024 as against 4.4 percent in 2023. Emerging and 
Developing Asia’s growth dipped to 5.3 percent in 2024 
compared to 5.7 percent in 2023 due to a drop in the 
growth of China and India with growths of 5.0 percent and 
6.5 percent in 2024 as against 5.2 percent and 8.2 percent 
in 2023 respectively. Sub-Saharan Africa experienced 
marginal growth of 4.0 percent in 2024 compared to 3.6 
percent in 2023.
The recent global inflationary experience was characterized 
by a complex set of events. During COVID-19 lockdowns, 
demand shifted towards goods and pivoted towards 
services as economies reopened. These demand 
shifts occurred in the context of supply disruptions 
and unprecedented fiscal and monetary stimulus. 
Subsequently, the Ukraine and Russian war led to spikes 
in commodity prices. Headline inflation continued to 
ease in most countries through 2024, led by further falls 
in food, energy and goods price inflation; even though 
the services price inflation is still persistent, and was 
approximately 4.0 percent in the median for Organization 
for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) in 
September. Global headline inflation moderated from an 
annual average of 6.7 percent in 2023 to 4.5 percent in 
2024, with advanced economies returning to their inflation 
targets sooner than emerging markets and developing 
economies. Central banks in many countries, because of 
inflation, remained cautious in lowering policy rates. 
Global public debt is elevated and projected to exceed 
US$100 trillion in 2024 and will rise over the medium 
term. This implies that risks to the debt outlook are heavily 
tilted to the upside. Much larger fiscal adjustments than 
currently planned are required to stabilize or reduce debt 
with high probability.
ECONOMIC REVIEW REPORT FOR 2024
Sub-Saharan Africa
Sub-Saharan African countries implemented difficult 
and much needed reforms to restore macroeconomic 
stability, and while overall imbalances have narrowed, the 
pictured is varied. Economic activity, however, exceeded 
expectations in 2024 with regional growth reaching 4.0 
percent compared to 3.6 percent in 2023. In their efforts to 
reduce these imbalances, policymakers faced three main 
hurdles. The first hurdle was that regional average growth, 
projected at 4.0 percent in 2024, was generally subdued 
and uneven, although it is expected to recover modestly 
next year. Resource-intensive countries continued to 
grow at about half the rate of the rest of the region, with 
oil exporters struggling the most. The factors that are 
dampening growth include conflict, insecurity, drought, 
and electricity shortages. Second, both domestic and 
external financing conditions continued to be tight, with 
many countries unable to access or afford financing. Third, 
complex underlying pressures linked to high poverty, 
lack of inclusion and job opportunities, as well as weak 
governance, compounded by rapid increases in the cost 
of living. And short-term effects of macroeconomic 
adjustments have caused significant hardships in many 
countries. The resulting social frustration and political 
pressures made it increasingly difficult to implement 
reforms.
Monetary tightening has curbed inflation, which was 
within target in about half of the region. Significant fiscal 
consolidation by countries in the region has stabilized the 
average debt-to-GDP ratio, albeit at a high level. External 
positions have strengthened, with sovereign spreads 
narrowing and more countries returning to the Eurobond 
markets. However, challenges persist as inflation remains 
in double digits in nearly one-third of countries in the 
region including Angola, Ghana, Ethiopia and Nigeria, and 
above target in almost half of the region, particularly where 
monetary policy was not anchored by exchange rate pegs. 
The 2024 annual average inflation for Angola, Ghana, 
Ethiopia and Nigeria were 21.4 percent, 23.5 percent, 
23.9 percent and 24.7 percent respectively. Debt service 
capacity was low, and interest payments exceeding 20 
percent of revenues in almost one-quarter of the countries, 
..........................
 Source: World Economic Outlook, October 2024: Policy Pivot, Rising. Threats. OECD, Economic Outlook, Volume 2024, Issue 2.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 26 
The Ghanaian Economy
The overall GDP growth of the Ghanaian economy in 
2024 was 5.7 percent, with Non-oil GDP growth being 
6.0 percent, compared to 3.1 percent and 3.6 percent 
respectively in 2023. The growth was pushed by average 
growth of 2.8 percent, 7.3 percent, and 5.9 percent for 
agriculture, industry, and services respectively. 
At the close of the year December 2024, the trade account 
of Ghana recorded a surplus of USD5,035 million being 
6.0 percent of GDP compared to USD2,630 million 
and 3.5 percent of GDP in 2023. Total exports at the 
end of December 2024 stood at USD20,427.1 million, 
while total imports were USD15,391.5 million in 2024. 
The current account balance on the balance payments 
posted a surplus of USD3,581.7 million representing 
4.3 percent of GDP as against USD1,407 million and 1.8 
percent of GDP in 2023. There was an improvement in 
the capital and financial account balance at the close of 
December 2024, even though it recorded a deficit. The 
capital account balance moved from negative USD732.6 
million, that is -0.9 percent of GDP in 2023 to negative 
USD386.7 million, representing -0.5 percent of GDP in 
2024. The improvement was because of an increase in 
foreign direct investments which were USD1,737.8 million 
as at December 2024 compared to USD1,308.3 million in 
December 2023. The overall balance of payment threw a 
surplus of USDS3,059.5 million at the end of December 
2024, being 3.6 percent of GDP, as against USD518.4 
million, representing 0.7 percent of GDP at the close of 
December 2023.
International Reserves 
The net international reserves of Ghana increased to 
USD6,376.1 million at the close of December 2024 as 
against USD3,134.8 million in December 2023. Import 
cover showed an improved position to 2.9 months cover at 
the end of December 2024 compared to 1.7 months cover 
in December 2023. Gross international reserves, excluding 
encumbered assets and petroleum funds, reflected a 
significant build-up of USD 8,982 million at the end of 
December 2024 representing 4.0 months import cover 
compared to USD3,661.4 million for 2.7 months import 
cover at the close of December 2023. Ghana now holds a 
stronger reservegold position of 30.5 tonnes as a back-up 
for the Ghanaian Cedi at the end of December 2024 due 
to the Bank of Ghana’s gold purchase programme.
a threshold statistically consistent with a high probability of 
fiscal stress. Consequently, rising debt service burdens are 
eroding the resources available for development spending. 
Foreign exchange reserve buffers are often insufficient, 
and concerns about overvaluation and competitiveness 
persist. The average debt-to-GDP for Sub-Saharan Africa 
in 2024 stood at 59.7 percent. The external debt-to-GDP 
in the same period was 27.8 percent, while the domestic 
debt-to-GDP was 31.9 percent. 
Governments in the region faced a difficult balancing 
act in reducing macroeconomic vulnerabilities while 
also addressing development needs and ensuring that 
reforms are socially and politically acceptable. Countries 
with high imbalances had to implement large and 
frontloaded adjustments given tight financing constraints. 
Nevertheless, there were countries with more moderate 
imbalances that pursued smaller and more gradual 
policy adjustments. But even countries with relatively low 
imbalances needed to pay heed to rebuilding fiscal and 
external buffers, as conditions allowed. Evidence shows 
that countries with stronger fundamentals, including 
monetary and fiscal stability, and sounder institutions, 
such as Cote d’Ivoire and South Africa have been more 
resilient to external financial shocks. Moreover, foreign 
exchange pressures have largely abated in some countries 
in the region since the end of 2023 and into 2024.
Reserves of Sub-Saharan Africa countries have dwindled 
lately after 4.7 months of import cover for goods and 
services in 2022 to 4.1 months in 2024. Likewise, the 
external current account, including grants, worsened after 
dipping from -2.7 percent of GDP in 2023 to -3.2 percent 
of GDP in 2024.

 27 ANNUAL REPORT
on the Insurance Industry
Public Debt
The total public debt of Ghana as at the close of December 
2024 stood at GHS726.7 billion compared to GHS610.0 
billion at the end of December 2023. In USD terms, the 
total debt at the end of December 2024 stood at USD49.4 
billion as against USD52.4 billion at the close of December 
2023. The total debt-to-GDP ratio closed at 61.8 percent 
in December 2024 compared to 68.7 percent in 2023. 
The total debt split into domestic and external debt 
components with the domestic debt standing at GHS309.7 
billion and the external debt being GHS416.8 billion at the 
end of December 2024. This translates into a debt-to-GDP 
ratio of 26.3 percent and 35.5 percent, respectively. The 
increase in the total public debt in 2024 was because of 
an increase in domestic debt since the Government could 
not borrow from the International Market due to the Debt 
Exchange Programme. The government was only confined 
to the domestic market for its borrowing to meet public 
expenditure due to a fall in external loans and grants. Total 
revenue and grants dropped to 15.9 percent of GDP at the 
end of December 2024 compared to 16.2 percent of GDP 
in 2023. Domestic revenue and tax revenue dropped by 0.1 
percent and 0.2 percent of GDP respectively at the close of 
the year 2024 and 2023 respectively. On the other hand, 
total expenditure increased by 1.6 percent of the GDP at 
the end of the year 2024.
Inflation
Year-on-year inflation has not seen a major decline but 
fluctuated since December 2023 after closing at 23.2 
percent. Inflation picked up marginally in January 2024 to 
23.5 percent, but far lower than the same period in 2023, 
which stood at 53.6 percent. Year-on-year inflation went up 
in March 2024 to 25.8 percent and further to 25.0 percent 
in April 2024. Thereafter, inflation trended downwards 
to 22.1 percent in October 2024. However, in November 
2024 it picked up to 23.0 percent and closed the year 
at 23.8 percent in December 2024. This increase in the 
inflation rate could be partly attributed to food prices. The 
Monetary Policy Committee (MPC) of the Bank of Ghana, 
to contain the inflationary trend, maintained the policy rate 
at 29.0 percent from the beginning of the year 2024 until 
September 2024 when it was reviewed downwards to 27.0 
percent.
Interest Rates
Interest rates were up from the first to the fourth quarter 
of 2024, responding to the monetary policy rate. The 
interbank weighted average rate and the average 
lending rate were up to 28.84 percent and 30.62 percent 
respectively in September 2024. However, from October 
2024 both the interbank weighted average rate and 
the average lending rate dropped to 27.69 percent and 
30.45 percent respectively. Comparatively, the interbank 
weighted average rate and the average lending rate closed 
the year in December 2024 at 27.03 percent and 30.25 
percent respectively, and 30.19 percent and 35.85 percent 
in 2023. The Ghana Reference Rate also dropped from 
30.45 percent in March 2024 to 29.31 percent in December 
2024. This explains the fall in the interbank weighted 
average rate and the average lending rate during the latter 
part of the year 2024, because the Ghana Reference Rate 
serves as the base rate for pricing loans by banks.
Treasury instruments rates also trended moderately 
downwards even though they were still high from the year 
2023 through to the year 2024 given the efforts put in by 
the Central Bank to contain inflation. The 91-Day Bill, 182-
Day Bill and 364-Day Bill traded at 26.40 percent, 28.90 
percent and 29.50 percent respectively in March 2024. 
The treasury bill rates dropped marginally until December 
2024 when they picked up with the 91-Day Bill trading at 
27.73 percent, 182-Day Bill at 28.43 percent and 364-Day 
Bill at 29.95 percent. 
Deposit rates for demand deposits and savings deposits 
remained constant at 2.63 percent and 5.0 percent 
respectively throughout the year 2024. Again, the time 
deposit rate for a 3-month period was 10.5 percent for the 
whole year of 2024. While the 6-month period deposit rate 
commenced the year at 11.0 percent through to August and 
dropped to 10.5 percent from September to December 
2024.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 28 
Exchange Rates
The Ghanaian cedi depreciated against the US Dollar, Euro 
and the Great British Pound in 2024. In February 2024 
the US Dollar traded at USD1.00 to GHS12.4642 with yearto-date depreciation of 4.7 percent, the British Pound at 
GBP1.00 to GHS15.8022 and year-to-date depreciation of 
4.2 percent and, the Euro traded at EUR1.00 to GHS13.5234 
and a year-to-date depreciation of 2.9 percent. The 
exchange rate fluctuations and the depreciation of the 
Ghanaian Cedi against the major currencies continued 
with the major depreciation occurring in October 2024. 
The year-to-date depreciation of the Ghanaian Cedi 
against US Dollar in October 2024 was 27.1 percent with 
USD1.00 selling at GHS16.3000. The Ghanaian Cedi 
depreciated on year-to-date basis against the Great 
British Pound by 27.8 percent and sold at GHS20.9700 to 
GBP1.00 and, the Ghanaian Cedi depreciated against the 
Euro by 25.8 percent and sold at GHS17.6992 to EUR1.00 
in October 2024. The increase in the depreciation was due 
to high demand for foreign currencies for the importation 
of goods in anticipation of the Christmas festivities and 
speculation by forex traders. However, the Ghanaian 
Cedi gained relative stability by the end of the year 2024 
with lowered depreciation rates. The Ghanaian Cedi in 
December 2024 sold for USD1.00 to GHS14.7000 and 
depreciated by 19.2 percent against the US Dollar, sold 
for GBP1.00 to GHS18.4008 with a depreciation of 17.8 
percent against the British Pound and the Euro went for 
EUR1.00 to GHS15.2141 and depreciated by 13.7 percent in 
December 2024.
Developments on the Ghana Stock Exchange Market
The Ghanaian Stock Exchange Market Composite Index 
(GSE-CI) closed trading in 2024 with 4,888.50 points 
returning 56.17 percent being the highest since 2013, 
compared to 3,130.23 points in December 2023. This 
impressive performance of the Index came mainly from 
the ICT sector with a volume of 920,797,718 shares followed 
by the Finance sector with 53,584,312. Consequently, 
the values of trade in the ICT and Finance sectors were 
GHS1,522,858,843.35 and GHS74,221,260.09 respectively. 
The Financial Stock Index made a gain of 25.20 percent, 
the highest since 2017. The top five gainers for the year 
were Unilever Ghana Plc (140.44 percent), Ecobank 
Transnational Inc. (106.67 percent), GCB Bank Plc (87.35 
percent), NewGold (78.72 percent), and MTN Ghana (78.57 
percent). The top losers were CAL Bank Ghana Plc (-27.08 
percent), Enterprise Group Plc (-17.15 percent), Meridian 
Marshalls Holding Company (-9.09 percent), Dannex 
Ayrton Starwin Plc (-5.00 percent), and SG Bank Plc (-4.46 
percent).
The Ghana Fixed Income Market (GFIM) concluded the 
year with a total traded volume of 174 billion, a 76.76 
percent increase from the 98.44 billion traded during the 
same period last year. Short-term government instruments 
accounted for 69.19 percent of volumes traded, followed by 
long-term government securities (28.76 percent), corporate 
bonds (1.34 percent) and Bank of Ghana securities (0.71 
percent).
The cumulative volume traded amounted to 992,188,948, 
valued at GHS2,153,054,280.73, indicating an increase of 
71.16 percent and 163.15 percent, respectively, compared 
to the same period last year. Market capitalization also 
increased, rising by 50.70 percent from GHS73,893.17 
million at the end of December 2023 to GHS111,356.09 
million by the close of December 2024.
The volume of trade in the Insurance sector picked up 
8,886,426 shares in 2024, compared to 1,947,985 shares 
in 2023. Consequently, the value of trade also increased to 
GHS9,771,290.28 in 2024 as compared to GHS982,639.59 
in 2023. The breakdown of the volume and value of shares 
traded for the only two insurance companies listed on 
the Ghanaian bourse, Enterprise Ghana Limited’s trade 
volume in 2024 was 6,983,373 shares with a value of 
GHS9,290,037.19. While SIC Insurance Plc traded a volume 
of 1,903,053 shares with a value of GHS481,253.09. 
The Ghanaian Insurance Industry should take advantage 
of the impressive performance the Ghana Stock Exchange 
and list on the bourse to raise more capital to support their 
operations.

Economic Outlook for 2025
The global economy is expected to grow at 3.3 percent 
in both 2025 and 2026, below the historical average of 
3.7 percent from the year 2000 to 2019. This is primarily 
on account of an upward revision in the United States 
of America offsetting downward revisions in other major 
countries. Global headline inflation is expected to decline 
to 4.2 percent in 2025 and to 3.5 percent in 2026, 
converging back to earlier targets in advanced economies 
than in emerging markets and developing economies. 
Likewise, the unemployment rate is projected to drop to 
5.2 percent in 2025 compared to 5.3 percent in 2024. 
The world economy appears to be slowly finding the 
right balance between a return to growth and controlling 
spiraling inflation. 
After four years of crisis many countries in Sub-Saharan 
Africa are not yet out of the woods, and the region faces 
yet another shock in the form of an abrupt shift in the 
external economic landscape as governments around the 
world reorder their policy priorities, including in particular 
a series of sizeable tariff measures by the United States, 
and countermeasures by trading partners. Growth in SubSaharan Africa is now expected to ease to 3.8 percent in 
2025 and 4.2 percent in 2026, a downward revision of 0.4 
percentage point and 0.2 percentage point, respectively. 
The slowdown has been driven in large partly by turbulent 
global conditions, as reflected in lower external demand, 
subdued commodity prices, and tighter financial conditions, 
with more significant downgrades for commodity exporters 
and countries with larger trade exposures to the United 
States. The median headline inflation reached 4.5 percent 
in early 2025, while the median debt-to-GDP ratio held 
steady below 60.0 percent. 
The International Monetary Fund projects a real GDP 
growth rate of 4.0 percent for the Ghanaian economy 
in 2025 and 4.8 percent in 2026. The inflation target by 
Ghana is likely to be missed as IMF projects 17.5 percent 
as compared to the 11.9 percent in the 2025 annual 
budget, but with a sharp decline to 9.4 percent inflation 
rate in 2026 moving closer to a single digit. The debt-toGDP ratio of 61.8 percent in December 2024 is expected 
to increase to 66.4 percent by the close of 2025, before 
declining to 62.7 percent in 2026 and 55.0 percent in 
2028. The International Monetary Fund is optimistic that 
Ghana will meet all key benchmarks, including the 2028 
debt target, provided the country is committed to the 
programme conditions.

Sources: World Economic Outlook January, 2025

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 30 
Industry Performance
Introduction
The 2024 financial year marked a transformative period 
in Ghana’s insurance sector, as the industry transitioned 
from IFRS 4 to IFRS 17 framework. This forward-looking 
shift has redefined how insurers measure, recognize, 
present, and disclose insurance contracts. The aim is to 
enhance transparency, consistency, and comparability of 
financial statements of companies undertaking insurance 
contracts. Against this backdrop, the industry posted 
notable nominal growth in total assets, insurance revenue, 
and profit after tax, alongside improvements in overall 
balance sheet quality. However, real growth for the sector 
using 2022 CPI as the basis period showed a contraction 
in growth.
Industry Total Asset Growth Analysis (2023 vs 2024)
The Ghanaian insurance industry recorded a nominal 20% 
year-on-year growth in total assets, expanding from GHS 
16.42 billion in 2023 to GHS 19.42 billion in 2024. In real 
terms, total assets contracted, ending the period at -3% from 
GHS 13.36 billion to GHS 12.99 billion. This performance 
is reflective of the 23% average inflation for 2024.
Life insurers assets grew by 22% and -1% growth in nominal 
and real terms respectively, non-life recorded nominal and 
real growth of 13% and -9% respectively, Reinsurers recorded 
44% and 16% in nominal and real growth respectively.

Investment Assets:
“Investments”, which is the most important component 
of insurers assets, grew from GHS 3.48 billion in 2023 to

Insurance and Reinsurance Contract Assets:
Two new categories of assets that have emerged under 
IFRS 17 due to change in presentation are the insurance 
contract assets and reinsurance contract assets: Insurance 
Contract Assets (GHS 13.36 million) and Reinsurance 
Contract Assets (GHS 1.19 billion) replaced IFRS 4 
presentation of reinsurance share of insurance liabilities. 
Insurance contract assets represent the insurer’s rights 
to future cash inflows arising from insurance contracts. 
The reinsurance contract assets reflect a cedant’s (an 
insurer) right to receive future payments cash inflows 
from a reinsurer based on the reinsurance contract. 
Receivables:
Receivables dropped sharply by 70%, from GHS 1.80 billion 
in 2023 to GHS 526.85 million in 2024 zThis decline 
is primarily due to the presentation and measurement 
requirements under IFRS 17 rather than a fall in receivables. 
Previously, under IFRS 4, reinsurance share of insurance 
liabilities, including what is now classified as reinsurance 
contract assets, was reported as part of receivables. 
Property, Plant & Equipment and Cash:
Cash increased by 22%, from GHS 711 million to GHS 
884.83 million, signaling improved short-term liquidity 
buffers. Property, Plant & Equipment (PPE) also grew 
marginally by 13%.

Life Insurance Investment Mix Analysis – 2024
 Figure 7: Life Sector Investment Mix
Ghana Insurance Market Report for 2024

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 34 
Investment Composition
In 2024, life insurance companies maintained a 
conservative investment structure based on safety and 
availability of investments in the market. Total investments 
reached GHS 8.72 billion by the end of the year. Given 
the long-tail nature of life insurers’ liabilities, it is crucial 
for them to maintain a sufficient portfolio of long-term 
investment assets to match their liability profiles.
The current investment landscape highlights a need for 
further development of Ghana’s capital market, particularly 
the stock market. This includes the introduction of 
collective investment schemes to provide life insurers with 
a broader range of alternative instruments, enabling more 
diversified investment portfolios. 
The primary constituents of these investments were:
Government Securities – These remained the cornerstone 
of life insurance investment portfolios, representing 
approximately 42.2% of total investments, or GHS 3.68 
billion. This allocation comprises both long-term and 
short-term government debt instruments. The continued 
dominance of government securities, even after the 
Domestic Debt Exchange Programme (DDEP), suggests 
either a renewed confidence in Government of Ghana 
instruments or, more likely, a reflection of the limited 
availability of alternative long-term investment options in 
the country
Property Investments (excluding PPE) - Investment in 
Properties or Real estate continued to be a significant 
component of life insurance companies’ portfolios, 
making up 20.9% (GHS 1.83 billion) of total investments. 
This reflects the life sector’s long-term investment horizon, 
as property assets generally offer inflation-hedged returns 
and stable values. However, it is important to note that 
these assets also come with inherent liquidity and 
valuation risks. A substantial portion of these gains are 
unrealized, which can have implications for an insurer’s 
immediate liquidity.
Term Deposits held at licensed banks - These constituted 19.6% (GHS 1.71 billion) of total investments, an increase of 
4.6% from 2023. These are investments in deposits with fixed maturity periods held at licensed banks.
Equities, both listed and unlisted- Listed (on the Ghana 
Stock Exchange) and unlisted equities collectively 
represented 13.4% (GHS 1.17 billion) of the investment 
portfolio. This proportional allocation remained consistent 
with 2023, signaling a continued cautious approach to 
equity market volatility. Nevertheless, equities remain 
crucial for offering upside potential and enhancing portfolio 
diversification.
The remaining investment instruments: The remaining 
portion of the portfolio comprised Other Term Deposits 
(2.4%), Mutual Funds (0.3%), Related Party Investments 
(1.0%), and Other Investments (0.1%). These minimal 
exposures likely reflect strategic de-risking initiatives and 
adherence to prudential limits. These lower allocations 
are consistent with the National Insurance Commission’s 
(NIC) stance on minimizing exposure to high-risk or illiquid 
instruments, particularly those involving related parties.

Non-Life Insurance Investment Portfolio Analysis – 2024
In 2024, the Non-Life insurance sector maintained a diversified investment portfolio, prioritizing liquidity and secure 
assets due to the short-tail nature of its insurance contract obligations. While short-term instruments were favored, 
equities and property continued to play significant roles.
The primary constituents of the Non-Life insurance investment portfolio were:
Government Securities: These remained the cornerstone 
of investment allocations, representing 31.9% of total 
invested assets (GHS 1.34 billion). This marks an increase 
from 27% in 2023, affirming the sector’s continued 
preference for sovereign-backed instruments that support 
regulatory capital and carry low default risk.
Equities: Both listed and unlisted equities accounted for 
22.8% (GHS 957.51 million), maintaining a strong presence 
in Non-Life portfolios. This indicates that equities 
remain an important component of insurers’ investment 
strategies, offering potential for higher returns despite their 
characteristic of higher volatility.
Deposits held at licensed banks: These comprised 22.3% 
(GHS 934.99 million), a marked increase from the 2023 
figure of GHS 584.8 million. 
Property Investments: These constituted 16.7% (GHS 
700.6 million), largely unchanged from the prior year. While 
property remains a valuable store of long-term value, its 
illiquidity and valuation uncertainty continue to draw 
regulatory attention, especially for short term insurers. A 
substantial portion of these gains are unrealized, which 
can have implications for an insurer’s immediate liquidity.
Other Asset Classes: Other asset classes, including 
Corporate Debt (2.0%), Other Term Deposits (1.8%), Mutual 
Funds (0.5%), Related Party Investments (1.7%), and Other 
Investments (0.3%), collectively made up less than 7% 
of total investments. Their relatively marginal presence 
suggests insurers are exercising prudence in areas with 
potentially higher risk or capital charges for solvency.

 37 ANNUAL REPORT
on the Insurance Industry
Market Leaders
As part of the NIC’s prudential mandate of ensuring the financial health and soundness of the insurance market, key 
performance indicators (KPIs) have been refined to better reflect the recognition, measurement, presentation and 
disclosure requirements under IFRS 17. These indicators now serve as forward-looking KPIs that provide insights into 
financial strength and profitability, thereby supporting more effective supervision and market oversight.
The core performance areas currently monitored include:
1. Insurance Revenue
2. Insurance Service Result (formerly Underwriting 
Profit) 
3. Profit After Tax
4. Total Assets
5. Claims Incurred
6. Contractual Service Margin (CSM)
Under IFRS 17, the insurance industry has transitioned 
from recognizing gross revenue (Gross Written Premiums) 
to reporting Insurance Revenue. This change is designed 
to more accurately reflect the value of insurance services 
provided over the period of coverage, aligning revenue 
recognition with the delivery of services rather than just the 
receipt of premiums.
Similarly, the concept of Underwriting Profit has been 
redefined and expanded into the Insurance Service Result 
(ISR). The ISR now provides a more comprehensive view of 
an insurer’s core operating performance by incorporating:
• Fulfilment cash flows: These represent the 
expected future cash outflows associated 
with fulfilling insurance contracts (e.g., claims, 
expenses).
• Risk adjustments: These account for the 
uncertainty inherent in the measurement of future 
cash flows.
• Systematic release of unearned profits 
(Contractual Service Margin - CSM for life insurers): 
The CSM represents the unearned profit an 
insurer expects to earn over the life of a group of 
insurance contracts. Under IFRS 17, this profit is 
recognized systematically in the Insurance Service 
Result as services are provided.
This fundamental shift in accounting is expected to 
significantly enhance the comparability and transparency 
of financial statements among regulated entities. It 
will allow for a more consistent interpretation of insurer 
performance across the industry and over time, providing 
stakeholders with a clearer and more reliable picture of an 
insurer’s financial health and operational efficiency.
Contractual Service Margin (CSM) and Life Sector 
Reporting
Contractual Service Margin (CSM) increased significantly 
from GHS 1.72 billion in 2023 to GHS 2.55 billion in 2024, 
representing a 48% year-on-year growth. This sharp rise 
reflects the accumulation of unearned future profits on inforce insurance contracts under IFRS 17, particularly within 
the life sector where long-duration contracts dominate. 
The increase may be attributed to higher volumes of 
new business, improved pricing adequacy, or changes 
in assumptions relating to fulfillment cash flows, risk 
adjustments, and expected future service performance.
Figure 12.: Market Leaders – CSM (GHS’million)

Profit After Tax – Trends and Sectoral Contribution (2020–2024)
The insurance industry has seen steady improvements 
in bottom-line performance over the past five years. 
Aggregate Profit After Tax (PAT) rose consistently across 
both the Life and Non-Life sectors, reflecting improved 
underwriting, investment returns, and overall operational 
efficiency.
Overall Growth in PAT
Industry-wide PAT increased from GHS 293 million in 
2020 to GHS 1.24 billion in 2024, representing a compound 
growth of approximately 324% over the five-year period.
In 2024, industry PAT rose by 40%, from GHS 886 million 
in 2023 to GHS 1.24 billion, despite macroeconomic 
pressures and ongoing business model adjustments 
under IFRS 17.
Life vs. Non-Life Sector Performance
The life sector has consistently contributed a larger share 
to industry’s PAT in recent years. In 2024, the life sector 
recorded GHS 709 million, accounting for approximately 
57% of total industry PAT. The non-life sector reported 
GHS 532 million in 2024, making up the remaining 43%
of total industry PAT. This marks a gradual shift in balance: 
from a life sector share of 75% in 2020, down to 57% in 
2024, as non-life profitability strengthened.
Growth Dynamics (2023–2024)
Non-life PAT grew by 43% (from GHS 370 million in 2023 to 
GHS 532 million in 2024), largely reflecting improvements 
in insurance service results, stronger investment income, 
and effective cost containment.
Life PAT grew by 37% (from GHS 516 million to GHS 709 
million), bolstered by the implementation of IFRS 17, where 
the proper release of the Contractual Service Margin 
(CSM) and clearer presentation of service results may have 
contributed to enhanced earnings stability.

Market Leader – Total Assets
The 2024 assessment of asset concentration across 
the insurance industry highlights a continued trend of

highly consolidated market structure where the financial 
health and risk management practices of a few firms have 
significant implications for overall sector stability.

distribution. The top five (5) Non-Life insurers controlled 
50% of sector assets, and the top 10 accounted for 73%, 
reflecting a relatively more competitive landscape with 
room for mid-tier and smaller insurers to scale up.

2024 LIFE INSURANCE CLASS OF BUSINESS ANALYSIS
Life Insurance Business Mix (Class of Business Analysis)
The 2024 breakdown of insurance revenue across classes 
of life insurance business offers a revealing snapshot of 
market preferences, insurer focus, and emerging growth 
sectors. Under the IFRS 17 framework, this data reflects 
income from insurance services rendered during the 
reporting period, offering a clearer picture of how each 
class contributes to the delivery of value to policyholders.
Universal Life 
With GHS 564.99 million, Universal Life emerged as the 
highest revenue-generating class in 2024, accounting for 
a substantial portion of overall life business. Its appeal lies 
in its hybrid structure, combining death benefits with an 
investment savings component, along with flexibility in 
premiums and benefits. 
WholeLife and Endowment
Whole Life and Endowment contributed GHS 319.38 
million and GHS 276.96 million respectively and continued 
to post strong revenue figures, highlighting the resilience of 
traditional protection and savings products.
Investment-Linked Policies 
Revenue from Investment-Linked Policies (excluding unitlinked) totaled GHS 270.71 million, placing them among the top five business lines. Unit-Linked Policies contributed 
only GHS 18.63 million.
Group and Credit Life 
The Group Life sector generated GHS 386.63 million, 
reflecting large-scale institutional policies. Meanwhile, 
Credit Life followed closely with GHS 184.79 million, 
benefitting from increased lending activity and lenderdriven policy bundling across microfinance, consumer 
credit, and banking sectors. 
Term Life 
Term Life business brought in GHS 249.64 million, indicating demand for pure risk protection policies. 
Microinsurance 
With GHS 49.91 million in revenue, Microinsurance 
continues to demonstrate growth potential. The sector 
reflects broader inclusion efforts, especially among informal 
sector workers, low income and rural populations, often 
in partnership with telcos and Micro Finance Institutions 
(MFIs).

2024 Non-Life Insurance Class of Business Analysis
Motor insurance continues to dominate the non-life 
sector, contributing significantly to industry-wide revenue. 
Motor Comprehensive alone generated GHS 1.48 billion, 
making it the single largest class in the non-life portfolio. 
Motor Third Party followed with GHS 453.23 million. Other 
motor-related products, including Motor Others and 
the Motor Third Party, Fire & Theft hybrid class, brought 
in GHS 85.96 million and GHS 9.08 million respectively. 
Altogether, motor-related policies contributed nearly GHS 
2.03 billion in insurance revenue, reinforcing the need for 
continuous enhancement of the Motor Insurance Database 
(MID), which has improved compliance with the tariff and 
reduced premium undercutting.
Fire, Theft & Property (Commercial) generated GHS 1.45 
billion, ranking as the second highest revenue-generating 
class after Motor Comprehensive while Fire, Theft & 
Property (Private), contributed GHS 64.10 million. 
The Engineering class generated GHS 170.58 million, while 
Other Engineering lines brought in GHS 148.52 million. 
Bonds contributed GHS 302.99 million in insurance 
revenue.
Moderate contributions were observed from marine, 
aviation, and statutory employer risk classes. Marine Cargo 
produced GHS 145.91 million in revenue, while Marine Hull 
added GHS 64.46 million. Aviation insurance recorded 
GHS 39.69 million. Workman’s Compensation added GHS 
52.03 million to the sector’s revenue.
Accident and liability lines remained integral to the nonlife product mix. Accident (Others) generated GHS 225.43 
million, while Accident (Public Liability) brought in GHS 
174.71 million. Accident (Personal) and Professional 
Indemnity contributed GHS 40.47 million and GHS 16.04 
million respectively. Travel Insurance recorded GHS 23.28 
million. 
Despite their importance to financial inclusion and 
resilience-building, agricultural insurance and non-life 
microinsurance remain underdeveloped. Agric - Area 
Index recorded GHS 1.96 million, Agric- Weather Index 
contributed GHS 410,882, and Agric- Others accounted 
for only GHS 126,100. There was no reported revenue for 
Poultry or Non-Life Microinsurance. This underlines a 
significant gap in product penetration, particularly in rural 
and informal part of Ghana where climate-related risks, 
livestock mortality, and low-income vulnerability remain 
high. 
Other Approved Products
Other Approved Products contributed GHS 10.74 million, indicating marginal activity in niche or insurer-specific product 
innovations that fall outside traditional classifications.

NATIONAL INSURANCE COMMISSION 52 
The NIC undertook the 2024 edition of the Public 
Perception, Awareness, and Confidence (PPAC) Survey to 
help measure performance. This is part of the NIC’s annual 
research initiative to capture the experiences, opinions, 
and attitudes of Ghanaians toward insurance—from 
traditional life and motor policies to emerging products like 
microinsurance and digital insurance products.
The survey shows the level of commitment of NIC to 
transparency, accountability, and inclusive development 
within the sector. The 2024 PPAC Survey builds on the 
foundation laid by the previous edition conducted from 
2019 to 2023, offering a unique opportunity to track 
progress, identify emerging challenges, and evaluate the 
impact of reforms and public education campaigns rolled 
out in the intervening years. This evidence-based approach 
ensures that regulatory and policy decisions are grounded 
in real-world insights, ultimately serving the best interests 
of both consumers and providers. 
The overarching goal of the 2024 PPAC Survey was to 
deepen understanding of how insurance is perceived, 
understood, and experienced by the Ghanaian public.
